## Document 009: 1亿桶大缩水？报道称欧盟认为释放油储计划主要兑现此前承诺、并非新增份额

Source PDF: all report pdf/1亿桶大缩水？报道称欧盟认为释放油储计划主要兑现此前承诺、并非新增份额.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

G7 成员国上周五同意释放最多 1 亿桶原油和柴油储备。 IEA 3 ⽉曾宣布 4 亿桶释放计划，截
⾄上周五，其中仍有约 7500 万桶尚未释放。欧盟成员国普遍认为，此次⾏动只是在落实先
前的承诺，⽽⾮追加新的份额。对于上周 G7 协议中特别强调的柴油库存提前释放，欧盟成
员国认为可以实施，但规模可能有限。
摘要：
国际能源署（ IEA ）与欧盟成员国周三就释放⽯油和柴油战略储备展开讨论。市场关注七国集团
（ G7 ）上周宣布的最多 1 亿桶储备释放计划究竟能带来多少新增供应。
欧盟成员国普遍认为，此次⾏动很⼤程度上将兑现今年 3 ⽉已经作出的释放承诺，⽽⾮额外释放
1 亿桶新的⽯油库存。
据媒体报道， IEA 成员国周三下午召开临时会议，讨论加快落实 3 ⽉宣布的战略⽯油储备释放计
划。 IEA 会后表⽰，成员国⽀持 “ 加快释放 3 ⽉宣布的⽯油库存 ” ，并⽀持 “ 在可能的情况下优先释
放柴油库存 ” 。
与此同时，欧盟能源⼯作组也于周三举⾏电话会议，进⼀步讨论 G7 上周达成的储备释放安排。

欧盟成员国官员普遍认为，任何进⼀步释放都应控制在 3 ⽉已经确定的规模之内。部分成员国希
望 IEA 先对释放⾏动可能产⽣的市场影响进⾏评估。
柴油价格近期⼤幅上涨，伊朗战争以及针对俄罗斯炼⼚的袭击扰乱炼油和运输活动，并推⾼燃
料成本，进⽽给运输、农业等⾏业和通胀带来压⼒。
G7 宣布释放 1 亿桶，但新增供应规模仍存疑
G7 成员国上周五同意释放最多 1 亿桶原油和柴油储备。此次⾏动此前受到美国总统特朗普推动，
美国政府曾警告，如果欧洲国家不采取更多措施压低柴油价格，美国可能限制柴油出⼝。
不过， 1 亿桶究竟有多少属于真正新增的市场供应，⽬前仍不明确。
IEA 今年 3 ⽉曾宣布创纪录的 4 亿桶战略⽯油储备释放计划，以应对原油价格上涨和供应损失。截
⾄上周五， IEA 数据显⽰，其中约 3.25 亿桶已经释放，仍有约 7500 万桶尚未释放。
两名欧盟外交官表⽰，欧盟国家预计，这部分尚未释放的约 7500 万桶将计⼊ G7 上周宣布的 1 亿
桶计划。
摩根⼤通分析师也作出了类似判断。该⾏认为，上周 G7 声明中所提及的 “1 亿桶 ” 并不意味着市场

将获得 1 亿桶全新的战略储备供应，其中相当⼀部分实际上是完成 3 ⽉已经作出的释放承诺。
欧盟优先考虑兑现此前承诺
欧盟内部⽬前倾向于在 3 ⽉既定释放规模之内推进此次⾏动。
⼀份欧盟内部⽂件显⽰，欧盟成员国官员周三上午讨论后形成⼴泛共识，任何进⼀步释放战略
⽯油储备的⾏动都应维持在 3 ⽉已同意的规模以内。欧盟国家此前仅释放了 3 ⽉承诺规模的⼤约
⼀半。

写评论
图⽚
对于上周 G7 协议中特别强调的柴油库存提前释放，欧盟成员国认为可以实施，但规模可能有
限。没有欧盟国家明确承诺在 20 天窗⼝内额外释放柴油库存。
法国和意⼤利可能考虑在 3 ⽉承诺的基础上进⼀步释放部分柴油库存，但⽬前尚未作出最终决
定。
德国经济部则表⽰，德国将参与 IEA 今年 3 ⽉已经确定的进⼀步释放，但没有公布新的释放规
模。
波兰能源部⻓⽶沃什 · 莫蒂卡周三表⽰，如果达成共识，波兰已经准备好释放⽯油和柴油库存，
但预计 IEA 不会在当天就此作出最终决定。
IEA 仍有逾 11 亿桶库存
IEA 表⽰，其成员国⽬前仍持有相当于约 11 亿桶原油的⽯油库存，其中柴油库存超过 2 亿桶。
这意味着，尽管此次释放计划的实际新增规模存在较⼤不确定性， IEA 成员国仍拥有较⼤规模的

战略库存，可以在供应受到进⼀步冲击时进⾏⼲预。
此次储备释放的重点之⼀是柴油。受伊朗战争、俄罗斯炼⼚遭袭以及油轮运输受扰等因素影
响，柴油价格近期升⾄创纪录⽔平，并对卡⻋运输、农业等⾏业形成成本压⼒。
欧洲⽅⾯此前表⽰，⽬前并不存在燃料供应短缺⻛险，但柴油价格上涨已经演变为明显的价格
压⼒。

## Document 130: 9月PMI数据点评：经济景气修复覆盖面拓宽，企稳信号持续增强

Source PDF: all report pdf/9月PMI数据点评：经济景气修复覆盖面拓宽，企稳信号持续增强.pdf
Report source: 金融街证券
Date: 2026-09-30
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

本公司及作者在自身所知情范围内，与本报告中所评价或推荐的证券不存在法律法规要求披露或采取限制、静默措施的利益冲突。
本报告版权仅为本公司所有，未经书面许可，任何机构和个人不得以任何形式将研报内容和相关信息对外公布、转发、转载、传播、复制、编辑、修改、引用等。如有上述违法行为，本公司保留追究相关方法律责任的权利。
市场有风险，投资需谨慎。投资者不应将本报告作为作出投资决策的唯一参考因素，亦不应认为本报告可以取代自己的判断。在决定投资前，如有需要，投资者务必向专业人士咨询并谨慎决策。
9月制造业延续了8月制造业订单改善的态势，生产和采购进一步跟进，但需求没有继续加速，
就业、订单储备和经营预期也未同步改善。第一，PMI重返扩张区间主要由生产推动。 9月制造
业PMI50.1%，环比上升0.3pp；生产指数51.7%，环比上升1.3pp。按制造业PMI标准权重测算（制
造业PMI=30%新订单+25%生产+45%其他），生产指数上升贡献约0.325个百分点，基本解释了综合
指数0.3个百分点的回升。因此，9月景气改善的增量主要来自生产端。第二，内外需新订单基本

延续了8月的温和修复。9月PMI新订单指数50.5%，环比微降0.1pp，基本延续了8月的温和扩张。
其中，新出口订单50.0%，环比略降0.1pp；新内需订单50.7%（按新订单内需占比70%推算），同
样环比略降0.1pp，内外需制造业订单环节改善得到延续，终端需求是否跟进仍待验证。第三，
生产与采购加快，产成品继续去库，但尚未形成主动补库信号。 9月采购量指数51.0%，环比上
升0.5pp；进口指数49.2%，环比上升0.6pp。企业增加生产所需的采购活动，与生产扩张相呼应。
产成品库存指数47.6%，环比下降0.8pp；原材料库存指数虽微升至48.2%，但仍低于荣枯线，表
明企业备货仍较谨慎。新订单维持温和扩张，生产加快与产成品去库并存，呈现一定的被动去库
存特征；但在手订单指数46.0%，环比下降0.7pp，说明已有订单储备仍在消耗，当前改善也可能
包含前期订单集中交付的影响。后续能否转向主动补库，仍取决于新增订单持续扩张，以及企业
需求预期和盈利状况改善。第四，量价同步改善的信号增强，但企业利润修复仍面临成本约束。

9月原材料购进价格指数60.8%，环比上升4.2pp；出厂价格指数54.0%，环比上升3.6pp，说明投
入品和产成品涨价的企业覆盖面均有所扩大，出厂端价格传导较8月增强。但购进与出厂价格指
数之差为6.8pp，环比扩大0.6pp，成本压力仍需关注。第五，就业与预期没有跟随生产上行，
企业对持续扩张仍较谨慎。从业人员指数48.4%，环比下降0.3pp；生产经营活动预期指数53.8%，
环比持平。企业增加生产和采购，却尚未同步增加用工或上调经营预期，说明本轮修复更多体现
为现有产能利用和订单执行改善，向新增资本开支、就业和居民收入的传导仍不充分。
非制造业方面，商务活动指数50.2%，环比上升1.2pp；其中，建筑业指数50.3%，环比上升
3.4pp，服务业指数50.2%，环比上升0.9pp。建筑业较快回升，或与政策性金融工具逐步投放使
用、项目资本金和配套融资落实，以及施工活动加快有关，政策对投资的支撑可能开始显现。
综合看，9月制造业、建筑业和服务业景气同步改善，经济修复的覆盖面较8月扩大，经济

企稳的积极信号有所增强。制造业订单延续扩张、生产加快，建筑业明显回暖，为工业生产保
持韧性和政策支持领域投资边际修复提供了依据。但制造业在手订单继续收缩，非制造业新订
单仍低于荣枯线，说明当前活动回升尚需持续需求支撑。后续关键在于，财政资金和政策性金
融工具能否加快转化为项目建设与新增订单，并通过企业回款、传统行业盈利和就业收入改善，
逐步带动民间投资与居民消费恢复。
证券研究报告宏观简评

## Document 131: 9月PMI数据解读：9月制造业PMI回升至荣枯线之上

Source PDF: all report pdf/9月PMI数据解读：9月制造业PMI回升至荣枯线之上.pdf
Report source: 国新证券股份
Date: 2026-10-02
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

市场研究部 2026年 9月 30日
事件
9月 30日，国家统计局服务业调查中心公布，9月制造业采购
经理指数（PMI）为 50.1，非制造业商务活动指数为 50.2，综合
PMI 产出指数为 50.7。
解读
一、9 月制造业 PMI 小幅回升至荣枯线之上。
9月份，制造业采购经理指数（PMI）为 50.1%，比 8 月上升
0.3个百分点，时隔 2个月升至扩张区间。
从分类指数看，构成制造业 PMI 的 5 个分类指数中，生产指
数、新订单指数和供应商配送时间指数均高于临界点；原材料库存
指数、从业人员指数低于临界点。
生产指数为 51.7%，比 8月上升 1.3个百分点，表明制造业生
产活动扩张加快。
新订单指数为 50.5%，比 8月下降 0.1个百分点，高于临界点，
表明制造业市场需求继续改善。
原材料库存指数为 48.2%，比 8月上升 0.1个百分点，表明制
造业主要原材料库存量降幅略有收窄。
从业人员指数为 48.4%，比 8月下降 0.3个百分点，表明制造
业企业用工景气水平回落。

供应商配送时间指数为 50.1%，与 8月持平，高于临界点，表
明制造业原材料供应商交货时间继续加快。
9月主要原材料购进价格指数为 60.8，比 7月上升 4.2个百分
点，持续高于荣枯线，出厂价格指数为 54.0，比 7月回升 3.6个百
分点，上游原材料购进价格持续上涨，出厂价格继续上涨，企业效
益好转。
二、9 月非制造业 PMI 回升至荣枯线之上。
9月份，非制造业商务活动指数为 50.2%，比 8月上升 1.2个
百分点，时隔 2个月回升至荣枯线之上，非制造业景气水平明显回
升。
宏观研究
9月制造业 PMI回升至荣枯线之上
——9月 PMI数据解读
市场表现截至 2026.9.30
数据来源：国家统计局,国新证券整理。
分析师：郝大明
登记编码：S1490514010002
邮箱：haodaming@crsec.com.cn
证券研究报告

### Page 2

分行业看，建筑业商务活动指数为 50.3%，比 8月上升 3.4个
百分点；服务业商务活动指数为 50.2%，比 8月上升 0.9个百分点。
从服务业行业看，电信广播电视及卫星传输服务、货币金融服务、
保险等行业商务活动指数均位于 55.0%以上较高景气区间；资本市
场服务、房地产等行业商务活动指数均低于临界点。
新订单指数为 46.5%，比 8月上升 2.4个百分点，表明非制造
业市场需求景气水平回升。分行业看，建筑业新订单指数为 45.7%，
比 8月上升 3.3个百分点；服务业新订单指数为 46.7%，比 8月上
升 2.2个百分点。
投入品价格指数为 52.5%，比 8月上升 1.4个百分点，表明非
制造业企业用于经营活动的投入品价格总体水平继续上涨。分行业
看，建筑业投入品价格指数为 54.0%，比 8月上升 2.8个百分点；
服务业投入品价格指数为 52.2%，比 8月上升 1.1个百分点。
销售价格指数为 50.3%，比 8月上升 1.0个百分点，表明非制
造业企业销售价格总体水平有所回升。分行业看，建筑业销售价格

指数为 48.5%，比 8月下降 1.3个百分点；服务业销售价格指数为
50.6%，比 8月上升 1.4个百分点。
从业人员指数为 45.9%，比 8月上升 0.5个百分点，表明非制
造业企业用工景气度改善。分行业看，建筑业从业人员指数为
44.3%，比 8月上升 1.3个百分点；服务业从业人员指数为 46.2%，
比 8月上升 0.4个百分点。
业务活动预期指数为 55.5%，比 8月上升 0.5个百分点，表明
非制造业企业对市场发展信心有所增强。分行业看，建筑业业务活
动预期指数为 55.4%，比 8月上升 3.6个百分点；服务业业务活动
预期指数为 55.5%，与 8月持平。
三、9月综合 PMI 产出指数回升至荣枯线之上。
9月，综合 PMI 产出指数为 50.7%，比 8月上升 1.2个百分点，
升至临界点以上，我国企业生产经营活动总体较 8月扩张。
四、预测 10 制造业景气小幅上升。
9月制造业和非制造业业务活动预期指数分别为 53.8和 55.5，
企业对未来生产经营前景比较乐观，预测 10 月制造业景气小幅上

升，非制造业景气继续保持稳定。
投资建议
1：9月制造业和非制造业业务活动预期双双保持乐观。
2：电信广播电视及卫星传输服务、货币金融服务、保险等行
业商务活动指数均位于 55.0%以上较高景气区间。
3：9月制造业出厂价格指数继续上涨，制造业企业效益好转。

## Document 147: [005930] 삼성전자(005930) 현저한 저평가삼성전자(005930) 현저한 저평가현저한 저평가 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[005930] 삼성전자(005930) 현저한 저평가삼성전자(005930) 현저한 저평가현저한 저평가 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

00

삼성전자 (005930/KS) 
현저한 저평가 
3Q26 영업이익 106 조원 예상 
삼성전자의 3Q26 영업이익을 106조원 (+18% QoQ)으로 예상한다. 이는 기존 당사 
전망치 111 조원 대비 4.5% 하향된 것이다. 원/달러 환율 가정을 기존 1,450원에서 
1,400원으로 3.4% 하향, MX/NW 사업부의 적자 폭을 확대 반영 (-0.5조원 -> -
1.4조원)했기 때문이다. 주가의 핵심인 메모리 판가는 DRAM +17%, NAND +15%
로 오히려 예상을 소폭 상회할 것으로 예상된다. 출하는 수요 강세에도 불구하고 재고 
부족, HBM 확대에 따른 제한적 공급 여력으로 DRAM +4%, NAND +7%의 기존 예
상을 유지한다. HBM은 분기 최대 출하를 경신하기 시작할 것이다. 또한 AI 강세로 파
운드리 수주 및 가동률의 동반 상승이 지속 중이다. 성과 충당금 반영 등에 따라 단기 
흑자전환 가시성은 제한적이나, DS 사업부 전체적으로 강화되고 있는 국면이다.

2027 년 568 조원 (+55% YoY) 전망 유지. 환율 하락 반영 
SK증권은 삼성전자의 2027년 영업이익 전망치를 568조원으로 유지한다. 원/달러 환
율 가정을 기존 1,440원에서 1,340원으로 7% 하향했음에도, 실적 전망을 유지하는 
이유는 AI 메모리 수요 강세 속 공급 부족 심화, LTA에 따른 Dual market 효과에 의
한 Commodity 메모리 가격 강세, 2027년 HBM 판가 강세 (+103% YoY) 와 출하 
강세, 수익성 회복의 동반, 파운드리 적자 폭 축소의 본격화 전망 때문이다. 2027년 메
모리 출하는 DRAM +22%, NAND +16%, 판가는 DRAM +38%, NAND +28%
를 예상한다. 시장에서 우려하는 메모리 가격 하락, 감익 시나리오는 없을 것이다.

차별적 실적과 주주환원 본격화. 현저한 저평가 
SK증권은 메모리 업황과 삼성전자의 주가가 현저히 저평가 되어있다고 판단한다. 삼성
전자의 2027E P/E는 4.2X에 불과하며, 이익 창출력은 글로벌 최상위권이다. 주주환
원 정책 또한 기존의 FCF 50% 환원이 향후에도 유지될 것으로 가정한다면, 2027년 
193조원, 2028년 222조원으로 현재 시가총액 (우선주 포함) 대비 각각 11%, 13% 
수준에 육박한다. 성장을 위한 투자를 확대하고 있음에도, 대규모의 환원이 가능하다. 
LTA는 AI 시대 메모리 수요의 질과 실적 가시성을 제고시키며, 이는 주주환원의 지속
가능성을 의미한다. 메모리 가격의 높은 기저, LTA 물량의 계약 가격 적용을 감안하면, 
가격 상승률 자체는 안정화될 것이다. 다만 실적 안정성, 가시성, Duration을 확보하기 
시작했다는 점이 훨씬 중요한 가치이며, 아직 시장의 Playbook에 없을 뿐이다. 시대는 
변하고 있고, 시장도 변해야 한다. 투자의견 BUY, 목표주가 610,000원을 유지한다. 
2026-10-06

반도체. 한동희 / donghee.han@sks.co.kr / 3773-8826

## Document 158: A reprieve, not a recovery, for EU chemical producers

Source PDF: all report pdf/A reprieve, not a recovery, for EU chemical producers.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 13
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Iran war has hit EU chemicals less severely than feared,
with production, capacity utilisation and margins improving in
recent months. But renewed import competition will cloud the
outlook once Gulf oil and gas supplies to Asia recover. To reverse
the industry's long-term decline, producers and policymakers
must accelerate the green transition
Edse Dantuma
Polyethylene, the world's most common plastic, is used to make everyday
products like packaging ﬁlm, shopping bags, and bottles

Boost from softer Asian competition
The Iran war has hit European chemical producers less severely
than feared. After the ﬁrst tariﬀ shock in early 2025, demand
initially rose as customers brought forward purchases amid supply
chain uncertainty. The main boost, however, came from weaker
imports. Damage to Middle Eastern plants and Strait of Hormuz
blockades sharply reduced production and exports. About a quarter
of regional polyethylene capacity was damaged, with repairs likely

to take months.

### Page 2

Elsewhere in Asia, producers faced prolonged feedstock and energy
shortages. Asia depends far more on Gulf oil, gas and raw materials
than the EU does. Before the conﬂict, Asian countries sourced 40-
90% of their oil from the region, compared with around 20% for the
EU.
Earnings improve strongly, but production
returns to previous level
Reduced competition and tighter supply conditions drove three
consecutive months of growth in EU chemical production from

February to April, while sharp price rises provided a signiﬁcant
boost to second-quarter earnings. But the respite proved short-lived
as output contracted again as inventory restocking faded and
transport links through the Gulf improved.
By June, EU chemical production was 0.5% lower than a year
earlier and remained around 24% below early-2022 levels.
Meanwhile, exceptionally low Rhine water levels continue to disrupt
raw-material shipments and production schedules, particularly in

Germany and the Netherlands.
Modest 12-month production peak was
short-lived

### Page 3

*seasonally adjusted production, 2-month moving average
Source: Eurostat
Spain is Europe’s exception
Germany and the Netherlands have recorded the largest production
declines among the EU’s six biggest chemical producers. Together
with Belgium, they host Europe’s largest petrochemical and basic
chemicals cluster: the Antwerp-Rotterdam-Rhine-Ruhr Area. Highly
energy-intensive processes therefore weigh heavily on their
chemical sectors.
Spain stands out: chemical output has risen relatively strongly this

year and was the only top-six country to record growth in both
2024 and 2025. Its growth over the past decade, despite
ﬂuctuations, is also notable. Spain’s competitive position beneﬁts
from relatively low energy costs, a smaller share of basic chemicals,
a larger consumer chemicals segment, favourable domestic
demand and its location. A pipeline supplies Algerian gas, while EU
recovery funds have helped accelerate the expansion of renewable
power capacity.

Spain's chemical industry has held up
relatively well in recent years

### Page 4

*seasonally adjusted, 2-month moving average
Source: Eurostat
Temporary relief from competition and pressure on basic
chemicals
While the EU chemical industry has broadly been under pressure for
years, performance varies signiﬁcantly across segments. Four of
the ﬁve hardest-hit categories are in basic chemicals, which
produce base products and intermediates. Since early 2022, output
has fallen by between 25% in dyes and pigments and 47% in other
organic basic chemicals. The latter is particularly energy-intensive

and remains heavily reliant on fossil hydrocarbons for both energy
and feedstock. Compared with the previous downturn, basic
chemical producers beneﬁted from weaker international
competition in the ﬁrst half of this year. Only agricultural chemicals
fell sharply, as prolonged drought in the second quarter reduced
demand for crop protection products.
Consumer chemicals, including oils, extracts, perfumes and
toiletries, are the only segments to have grown both since early

2022 and since the start of this year. These downstream products
are less exposed to volatile energy prices and generate more value
through innovation. Speciality chemicals such as paints, coatings
and cleaning products sit between the two: output remains
structurally weak but has declined less than in basic chemicals.
Basic chemicals have been the hardest hit in
recent years

EU chemical activity follows the ebb and
ﬂow of the Iran war
The EU chemical production rebound was short-lived because of the
June US-Iran ceaseﬁre. Transport through the Strait of Hormuz
resumed and energy prices temporarily fell sharply. Chinese oil
reﬁning and exports of oil-based chemicals to the EU recovered. In
July, these exports were almost 40% higher than a year earlier,

while exports of organic basic chemicals – which account for about
two-thirds of China’s chemical exports to the EU – rose by nearly
50%.
Rebound of organics setting the tone for
chemical imports from China

### Page 6

Source: Eurostat
Production expectations rise as Middle East conﬂict ﬂares up
again
Since the conﬂict intensiﬁed again in July, Gulf oil, gas and
feedstock shipments crucial to Asian chemical activity have fallen
sharply. Although the switch from oil to coal oﬀers a partial
alternative, this is expected to increase pressure on Asian chemical
production and exports again, which will provide some relief to
European producers.
In August and September, most EU producers therefore expected to

raise output, with expectations reaching a two-year high in
September. European chemicals will beneﬁt from weaker
competition while transport remains disrupted. However,
alternative Gulf routes will probably gain importance if the conﬂict
persists, eventually reviving Asian import competition. Oil
shipments through Hormuz have recently increased to almost 80%
of pre-war levels.
Production expectations improve again
Production expectations for the months ahead, EU chemical industry

### Page 7

Source: European Commission
Speciality and consumer chemicals have the
strongest outlook
Order books improved slightly again after a brief dip in September.
Yet producers’ assessment of orders remains much further below its
long-term average (-26% versus -14%) than production
expectations, highlighting persistent structural weakness.
The historically wide gap between upstream and downstream
activities has narrowed this year, as have sentiment diﬀerences

between countries. Sentiment improved in basic-chemicals-heavy
Germany, Belgium and the Netherlands, but weakened in France,
Italy and Spain, where basic chemicals play a smaller role. This
reﬂects basic chemicals’ temporarily improved competitive position
but also continued weak demand.
European growth remains constrained by higher inﬂation and only
a limited recovery in end markets. Higher energy prices squeeze
consumer purchasing power, hitting consumer chemicals hardest.

Speciality chemicals have better prospects as investment rises and
the AI boom lifts demand for products and materials used in:
Sustainability and electriﬁcation, including battery materials,
chemical catalysts, ﬂame retardants, lightweight resins and
composites.
Defence equipment, including reinforced plastic ﬁbres,
advanced coatings and lubricants.

### Page 8

Semiconductors, including photosensitive polymers and ultra-
pure speciality chemicals and gases.
Higher energy and feedstock costs will bite
more gradually
Chemical prices closely track energy costs, so expensive and
volatile energy will also make them higher and more volatile. Yet
the steepest rise in selling prices appears to be over. Although most
producers expected to raise prices in August and September, the
expectations index was stable just below its long-term average – a

far milder increase than after the Iran war began. This suggests
supply is gradually expanding again and price competition is
increasing.
Greater diﬃculty passing on input-cost increases will put margins
under growing pressure in the second half of the year, reinforced as
higher energy costs feed through into purchase prices. Layered
hedging – through long-term contracts, futures and other
instruments – spreads this impact over time and helped margins

improve sharply in the ﬁrst half. Large chemical companies in
particular use rolling hedging strategies, ﬁxing or mitigating prices
for diﬀerent periods: from several months to several years,
depending on the product, company circumstances and market
outlook.
Selling price expectations stayed benign in
September
Selling price expectations for the months ahead, EU chemical industry

### Page 9

Source: European Commission
Structural problems will resurface
The European chemical industry’s structural problems are far from
over. Demand has been weak for years, while competitiveness
continues to erode. European energy prices are substantially higher
than in the US and China, and government support is much lower
than in China. Once transport ﬂows normalise, global overcapacity
will again drive an inﬂux of cheap imports, especially from China.

Global chemical capacity grew by about 15% between 2018 and
2023 and, based on projects already underway, is expected to rise
by another 10% through 2028, keeping structural overcapacity in
place. Over the past decade, the EU’s share of global chemical sales
halved to 13%, while China’s nearly quintupled to 46%. The EU’s
chemicals trade deﬁcit with China widened from €21bn in 2023 to
€24bn in 2025. An artiﬁcially weak Chinese yuan and US import
tariﬀs further undermine Europe’s competitiveness.

Restructuring wave is not over
Despite somewhat better market conditions this year, restructuring
is likely to continue. Most major players have announced strategic
interventions in recent years. Earlier this year, SABIC sold its
European petrochemical operations, while Evonik is implementing a
multi-year plan of divestments, closures and reorganisations. Ineos
plans to close three UK chemical plants, and Shell wants to sell its
entire chemicals division, including a major European cluster.

European chemical capacity shrank by 30 million tonnes, or 7%,
between 2022 and 2026. Announced plant closures increased
sixfold over this period, while investment fell sharply. Petrochemical

Climate costs pose a growing challenge
Climate costs also put Europe at a competitive disadvantage to
countries without carbon pricing or with much lower pricing. As free

allowances are phased out, EU ETS costs will probably weigh
increasingly on production costs towards 2030, both directly
through companies’ own emissions and indirectly through higher
electricity prices. In July 2026, the European Commission proposed
extending the emissions-reduction pathway, modestly easing the
cost increase, alongside more ﬁnancial support for clean-
technology investment.
CBAM oﬀers only partial protection for EU chemicals
Fertilisers and hydrogen are covered by the CBAM levy, which

protects against imports not subject to carbon costs. In
return, free allowances for CBAM products will be phased out

faster, reaching zero by 2034. This will widen the cost gap
between eﬃcient, electriﬁed installations and older fossil-
based plants. Besides the faster phase-out, CBAM has several
drawbacks for chemical companies:
The levy can be circumvented when a chemical feedstock
is covered by CBAM but a product containing it is not.
This may encourage imports of ﬁnished or intermediate
products from outside the EU. The European
Commission’s proposed expansion mainly targets

downstream steel and aluminium products, not the
wider chemical value chain.
Most chemicals remain outside CBAM. Chemical value
chains involve many processes, intermediates and
feedstocks, making embedded emissions diﬃcult to
calculate. Organic chemicals have therefore been
excluded for now.
CBAM does not oﬀset the EU ETS-related competitive
disadvantage faced by EU companies in export markets.
Promising decarbonisation projects remain operationally

challenging
To regain competitiveness, European plants must decarbonise
faster through carbon capture and storage, process electriﬁcation,
and recycled and bio-based feedstocks. Although projects are
regularly cancelled, promising initiatives remain. INEOS plans to
start Project One in Antwerp this year, introducing a modern
ethylene cracker that initially emits less than half as much CO ₂  as
the EU’s most eﬃcient cracker and becomes climate-neutral within

ten years.
Spain’s Repsol aims to decarbonise its Tarragona complex through
circular chemicals and methanol, large-scale green hydrogen and
oﬀshore CO ₂  storage. In September, fertiliser producer Yara began
shipping liquid CO ₂  from Sluiskil in the Netherlands to Norway for
the permanent underground storage of 800,000 tonnes of CO ₂  a

Such projects can quickly cost hundreds of millions of euros a year.

The €5bn Project One has contributed to the doubling of INEOS’s

Infrastructure and decarbonisation
investment are essential
Through its European Chemicals Industry Action Plan, the European

Commission aims to prevent essential chemical production from
leaving the EU. The European Critical Chemicals Alliance brings
policymakers and producers together to identify, monitor, protect
and support critical European production. Industry supports the EU
ETS goals and system, but argues that suﬃcient free allowances
should remain until eﬀective protection against carbon leakage and
adequate demand for sustainable products are in place.
This requires supportive EU policy. Europe’s chemical

transformation is becoming a race against time: more plants will
inevitably close, leaving only the most eﬃcient and fastest-
decarbonising companies and clusters. Brussels’ growing focus on
incentives and safeguards against unwanted international
dependencies therefore oﬀers the sector welcome support.

## Document 161: AIがサイバー攻撃を自動化する時代、サイバー株は上がるのか ～攻撃能力の急伸と企業業績・株価から検証する～

Source PDF: all report pdf/AIがサイバー攻撃を自動化する時代、サイバー株は上がるのか ～攻撃能力の急伸と企業業績・株価から検証する～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Research Report

2026 年 10 月 6 日

1/6 
AI がサイバー攻撃を自動化する時代、サイバー株
は上がるのか 
～攻撃能力の急伸と企業業績・株価から検証する～ 
 資産運用調査部 主席研究員  柏村 祐

（要旨） 
○ AI によるサイバー攻撃の自動化・高度化が現実のものとなっている。豪政府システムへのAI
侵入事件などが示すように、AI は与えられた目的を達成する過程で、許可された範囲を超え
てシステムにアクセスしうることが明らかになった。国内でも 9 月下旬以降、幅広い業種の
企業で不正アクセスの公表が相次いでいる。 
○ AI エージェントのサイバー課題解決能力はここ数年で急速に向上しており、サイバーセキュ
リティ企業の売上高・利益も足元で好調に推移している。 
○ 一方で、高度な AI モデルの登場は既存のサイバーセキュリティ製品の陳腐化リスクへの懸
念を生み、関連株が下落するというパラドックスも起きている。「サイバー株なら何でも上
がる」という安易な連想買いには注意が必要である。 
○ 個人投資家は、サイバーセキュリティセクター全体を丸ごと買うのではなく、「AIネイティ
ブ」なサイバーセキュリティ企業や、AIを活用した防衛技術を持つ巨大テック企業（メガテ
ック）への選別投資が求められる。

1. 豪政府システムへの「AI侵入事件」が鳴らす警鐘 
2026 年9月、オーストラリアのアルバニージー首相は、同国の公的医療保険制度（Medicare）
の統計ポータルサイトに、OpenAIのAIエージェントが無断で侵入していたと発表した。注目すべ
きは、これが悪意あるハッカーによるサイバー攻撃ではなく、OpenAIの研究チームがAIに調査を
実行させる過程で起きた点だ。AIは調査中にアクセス制限に直面すると、自ら制限を回避する方
法を見つけ出し、非公開のファイルにまでアクセスしたという。この事例は、AIが与えられた目
的を達成する過程で、許可された範囲を超えてシステムにアクセスしうることを示した。 
サイバー攻撃の脅威は、海の向こうだけの話ではない。日本でも9月下旬以降、物流、交通、
小売、カーシェアリングなど幅広い業種の企業が、不正アクセスによる個人情報流出の可能性を
相次いで公表している。これらの攻撃にAIが用いられたかどうかは明らかになっていないが、サ
イバー攻撃の影響が業種を問わず及んでいることは確かだ。政府も10月1 日から、能動的サイバ

### Page 2

2/6 
ー防御の一環として、重大なサイバー攻撃を未然に防ぐため、警察や自衛隊が攻撃元のサーバー
等にアクセスし、無害化措置を講じることを可能にした。 
AIがサイバー攻撃を自動化・高度化させる時代が到来しつつある。企業や政府の防御需要は急
増し、サイバーセキュリティ企業には特需がもたらされると期待される。個人投資家の間の「AI
の次なる恩恵銘柄はサイバー株」という見方にも、一定の説得力がある。しかし、 「サイバー株な
ら何でも上がる」と考えるのは早計である。AIによる攻撃の自動化・高度化は、攻撃件数を増や
すだけでなく、従来のセキュリティ製品や防御手法の有効性そのものを揺るがしかねないから
だ。本稿では、AIの攻撃能力の現在地と、それがサイバーセキュリティ企業の業績および株価に
与える影響を、データに基づいて検証する。そのうえで、 「サイバー株＝安全確実な成長株」とい
う見方をどう見直すべきか、個人投資家に向けて投資戦略を考えるための新たな視点を提示した
い。

2. AIの攻撃能力急伸とサイバーセキュリティ企業の業績・株価への影響 
(1) AIは人間なしでどこまで攻撃できるのか 
AIエージェントのサイバー攻撃関連能力は急速に高まっている。スタンフォード大学人間中心
AI研究所（Stanford HAI）が公表した「AI Index Report 2026」によれば、AIは人間が逐次指示
を与えなくても、専門的なサイバー課題を自律的に解ける水準に達しつつある。 
同レポートで紹介されている「Cybench」は、暗号、Webセキュリティ、リバースエンジニアリ
ング、フォレンジック、エクスプロイトなど、40の専門レベルのサイバーセキュリティ課題を評
価するベンチマークである。資料1 は、人間のガイドなし（unguided）でAIが課題を解決できた
割合の推移を示している。これを見ると、2024年時点ではわずか15％であった解決率が、2025年
には 82％へと急伸し、2026年には93％に達している。2年間で 78ポイントの上昇であり、AIが
自律的にサイバー課題を解く能力が大きく向上していることがわかる。

### Page 3

3/6 
資料 1 AI エージェントの自律的なサイバー課題解決能力は急伸している 
（注）サイバーセキュリティのベンチマーク「Cybench」における、人間のガイドなし（unguided）での課題解決率（％）の推移。
Cybench は暗号、Web セキュリティ、リバースエンジニアリング、フォレンジック、エクスプロイトなど 40 の専門レベル課題を評価
するもの。 
（出所）Stanford HAI, “AI Index Report 2026”をもとに第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

(2) サイバー需要は企業の売上・利益に届いているのか 
こうしたAIによるサイバー攻撃の脅威が高まるなか、防御側であるサイバーセキュリティ企業
への需要拡大が期待される。実際、その需要はすでに企業業績に表れている。 
Nasdaqが公表した「Q1 2026 HXRXL Cybersecurity Commentary」によれば、サイバーセキュリ
ティ関連銘柄で構成される「Nasdaq ISE Cyber Security Select Index（HXRXL） 」構成企業の
2026 年第1 四半期決算は極めて好調であった。同レポートによると、構成企業の売上高は前年同
期比で 11％増加し、純利益は同29.3％の増加を記録しており、売上高以上に純利益が大きく伸び
ていることが確認できる。さらに、市場予想に対する結果をまとめた資料2を見ても、売上高で
予想を上回った企業は指数ウェイトの92.2％を占め、純利益に至っては100％の企業が予想を上
回った。サイバーセキュリティ需要の強さは、売上高と利益の双方に表れている。

### Page 4

4/6 
資料 2 サイバーセキュリティ企業の業績は市場予想を大きく上回る 
（注）Nasdaq ISE Cyber Security Select Index（HXRXL）構成企業の 2026 年第 1 四半期決算の集計結果。市場予想に対する売上
高および純利益の予想超え・予想未達の企業数（指数ウェイトベース）、ならびに平均上振れ・下振れ率（％）を示す。 
（出所）Nasdaq, “Q1 2026 HXRXL Cybersecurity Commentary”をもとに第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

(3) それでもサイバー株は上がるのか 
業績が好調であれば株価も上昇すると考えるのが自然だが、 「AIによる攻撃高度化＝サイバー株
上昇」とは必ずしも言い切れない。AIは防御需要を増やす一方で、既存のサイバーセキュリティ
製品を陳腐化させるとの懸念も生んでいるからだ。 
国際決済銀行（BIS）が2026 年7月に公表したレポート「A Mythos moment? Frontier AI and 
cyber risk」は、このパラドックスを指摘している。2026年 4月にAnthropicが高度なサイバー攻
撃能力を持つAIモデル「Mythos」を発表した際、サイバーセキュリティ関連株は大きく下落し
た。資料3 は、Mythos発表前を0とした場合のサイバーセキュリティ関連株の累積異常リターン
（CAR）を示している。発表後、サイバーセキュリティETF（IHAK）や主要企業（CRWD、PANWな
ど）のCARは軒並みマイナスに転じ、一部の系列ではグラフ上で約−30％の大幅なマイナスを記録
した。BISは、この市場反応について「新しいAIモデルの登場により、既存のサイバーセキュリテ
ィ製品やサービスが陳腐化する（obsolescence）という投資家の懸念を反映している」との解釈
を紹介している。つまり、AIの進化はサイバーセキュリティ市場全体を拡大させる可能性が高い
ものの、同時にゲームチェンジを引き起こし、旧来の技術やサービスに依存する企業の競争力を
急速に奪うリスクを孕んでいる。

### Page 5

5/6 
資料 3 高度な AI モデルの発表後、サイバーセキュリティ関連株は下落した 
（注）2026 年 4 月の高度な AI モデル「Mythos」発表前後の、サイバーセキュリティ関連株の累積異常リターン（CAR、％）。発表前
を 0 として推移を示している。「Glasswing partners」は CrowdStrike（CRWD）および Palo Alto Networks（PANW）を指す。 
（出所）Bank for International Settlements, “BIS Bulletin No. 129: A Mythos moment? Frontier AI and cyber risk”をもとに第一ラ
イフ資産運用経済研究所作成

3. AI時代のサイバー株投資～「陳腐化リスク」を回避する新戦略～ 
第 2 章で見てきたように、AI はサイバー攻撃能力を飛躍的に高めている。また、足元のサイバ
ーセキュリティ企業の業績も好調である。しかし、最先端のAIモデルが登場した際には、市場は
既存のサイバーセキュリティ製品の「陳腐化リスク」に敏感に反応し、関連株は大きく売り込ま
れた。ここからいえるのは、サイバーセキュリティ市場全体は今後も拡大が見込まれるものの、
企業間の「勝ち負け」はこれまで以上に鮮明になるということだ。こうした環境変化のなか、個
人投資家には従来の投資アプローチを根本から見直すことが求められる。 
第一に、サイバーセキュリティセクター全体を丸ごと買うアプローチ（テーマ型ETFやインデ
ックスファンドなど）には注意が必要である。市場全体が成長しても、AIの進化に取り残され、
陳腐化して淘汰される旧世代の企業をポートフォリオに抱え込むリスクが高まるからだ。足元の
業績や過去の市場シェアだけで安心することはできない。第二に、個別銘柄を選別する際は、 「AI
ネイティブ」なサイバーセキュリティ企業であるかどうかが最大の焦点となる。攻撃側がAI を駆
使する以上、防御側もAIをシステムの中心に据え、未知の脅威を自律的に検知・遮断できるプラ
ットフォームを持っていなければ対抗できない。レガシーな製品を販売している企業ではなく、
クラウドベースで膨大なデータをAI で解析し、防御機能を継続的に高度化できる企業を見極める
必要がある。第三に、サイバー防衛の投資対象を「セキュリティ専業企業」以外にも広げること
である。 
最先端のAI モデルを開発し、強固なクラウドインフラを持つ巨大テック企業（メガテック）
は、自社のAI を活用した防衛技術を、顧客向けの強力なセキュリティサービスとして提供し始め

### Page 6

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
6/6 
ている。豊富な資金力とAI 開発力を持つメガテックを、サイバー防衛の担い手として評価し、ポ
ートフォリオの中核に据えることも、AI時代の投資戦略として有効な選択肢となるだろう。

## Document 166: AI脅威論にどう向き合うべきか

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI脅威論にどう向き合うべきか.pdf
Report source: DIR
Date: 2026-10-09
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

日本

株式会社大和総研 丸の内オフィス 〒100-6756 東京都千代田区丸の内一丁目 9 番 1 号 グラントウキョウ ノースタワー 
このレポートは投資勧誘を意図して提供するものではありません。このレポートの掲載情報は信頼できると考えられる情報源から作成しておりますが、その正確性、完全
性を保証するものではありません。また、記載された意見や予測等は作成時点のものであり今後予告なく変更されることがあります。㈱大和総研と大和証券㈱は、㈱大和
証券グループ本社を親会社とする大和証券グループの会社です。内容に関する一切の権利は㈱大和総研にあります。無断での複製・転載・転送等はご遠慮ください。

2026 年 10 月 9 日 全8 頁

AI 脅威論にどう向き合うべきか 
安全確保と開発競争の両立に向けた企業間協調の可能性と限界

AI アナリティックリサーチ室  主任研究員 田邉 美穂 
  主任研究員 新田 尭之

[要約] 
 最先端AI の急速な能力向上に伴い、AI がもたらす脅威への警戒が再び強まっている。
2026 年 9 月、Anthropic のダリオ・アモデイ CEO は、安全性の検証や対策が追いつく
範囲に開発速度を抑える必要性を訴え、企業間協調を提案した。企業や政府の間では、
開発ペースの調整や国際的な統制をめぐって立場が分かれる一方、能力評価やインシ
デント報告などでは協調が模索されており、9 月29 日には米国のAI 関連企業6 社が、
ホワイトハウスで安全確保に関する自主的な共同コミットメントに署名した。 
 AI 脅威論には、AI の高度化そのものがもたらす脅威と、AI の開発・保有・普及をめぐ
る脅威という二つの側面がある。前者には、人間による制御の喪失やサイバー攻撃な
どへの悪用があり、後者には、高度なAI 能力の一部の企業・国家への集中や、利用で
きる主体とできない主体間の格差拡大がある。前者に備えて単独で開発を減速すれば
他者に優位を奪われ、危険な能力へのアクセスを制限すれば後者の集中や格差を強め
かねない。安全確保と開発競争の間には、こうした二つのジレンマがある。 
 これらへの対応には企業や国家による協調が必要だが、その難しさは企業間と国家間
で異なる。企業間では他社も同等の安全対策を実施すると確認できれば、能力評価や
情報共有などで協調の余地がある。ただし、協調が機能するには①主要企業の参加、
②検証の信頼性、③副作用の抑制が求められるが、企業間の合意だけではこれらを満
たしにくい。さらに国家間では相手国の減速を確認しにくく、開発を続けることが合
理的になりやすい。このため企業間協調だけでは限界があり、企業間の安全基準や評
価方法を各国の制度に取り込み、各国の言語や利用環境における実効性を独立した立
場から検証できる主体を増やす必要がある。 
 日本に期待されるのは、こうした検証を担い、企業間で形成された安全基準を国際的
な枠組みへ接続する橋渡し役である。日本は広島AI プロセスを通じて国際指針や行動
規範の形成を主導してきたものの、AI セーフティ・インスティテュート（AISI）の本
格的な評価・検証体制の整備はこれからである。AI の能力向上と企業間協調をめぐる
議論が急速に進む中、最先端AI の能力・リスクに関する評価結果や安全対策の実効性
を自ら技術的に検証できる体制を、早急に整えることが求められる。

2 / 8

1. AI 開発の減速をめぐって割れる企業と政府 
アモデイ氏の問題提起 
2026 年9 月、Anthropic のダリオ・アモデイCEO は、最先端AI の能力向上が速すぎれば、安
全性の検証や対策が追いつかなくなるとして、主要企業が歩調を合わせて開発ペースを落とす
必要性を訴えた1。背景には、AI が次世代 AI の開発を加速させる再帰的自己改善の進展と、評
価中のAI エージェントが外部システムに侵入する事案が複数の企業で生じたことなどがある。
アモデイ氏は、人間による制御の喪失に加え、サイバー攻撃やバイオテロへの悪用、深刻な経
済的混乱などを主な脅威として挙げている。 
もっとも、アモデイ氏の主張は、AI の開発自体の停止を求めるものではない。むしろ、安全
性の検証に必要な時間を確保しながら開発を進めるため、①開発企業が外部評価者を受け入れ
る、②民主主義国のフロンティアAI 企業が政府の仲介や米国の独占禁止法（反トラスト法）の
限定的な適用除外の下で協調する、③国家間の協調へ広げる、という協調のあり方を提案して
いる。ただし、開発ペースの調整は米国が中国に対する技術的優位を維持できる範囲に限られ
るとし、対中輸出管理やモデル蒸留（他の AI モデルの回答を学習し、別の AI モデルの性能を
高める手法）への対策強化などを前提としている。

AI 企業各社と米国政府の反応 
この問題提起に対し、OpenAI やGoogle DeepMind などは企業間協調に理解を示した。また、
OpenAI のサム・アルトマン CEO は、開発速度を調整する必要性に同意し、独立した評価者に従
業員並みのアクセスを認めるというアモデイ氏の提案について、自社も同様に取り組む方針を
示した2。一方、Meta のマーク・ザッカーバーグCEO は、競争と法的責任が各社による安全確保
を促すとの考えを示し、NVIDIA のジェンスン・フアン CEO も AI のリスクを過度に強調する見
方に否定的な立場を取った3。ただし、Meta も第三者による独立評価には前向きであるほか 、
同社と NVIDIA はいずれも 9 月 29 日の共同コミットメント（後述）には署名している。各社の
立場は、安全確保か開発継続かで単純に二分できるものではない。 
こうした議論は企業間にとどまらず、国際安全保障上の問題へと広がった。9 月 23 日の国連
安全保障理事会では、アルトマン氏、アモデイ氏ら が AI と国際安全保障について意見を述べ
た。アルトマン氏は、人間が AI を制御できなくなることと、強力なAI が少数の企業・国家に
集中することを主要なリスクに挙げ、AI の能力とリスクの評価、人間による監督の維持、イン
シデント報告などに関する国際的な共通基準を求めた4。

これに対し、同じ会合で米国政府は、AI をめぐる国際対話には関与するものの、それが国際
的な統制の枠組みへ発展することには否定的な立場を示した。国際的な枠組みによって米国の
AI 開発が制約されることには否定的である5。 
もっとも、米国政府は、AI のリスクに対する限定的な協力や管理まで否定しているわけでは
ない。翌 24 日の米中首脳会談では、AI のリスクと便益について意見を交換する枠組みと、AI
インシデントに対応する二国間連絡チャネルを設けることで合意した6。開発ペースの調整や共
通規制には踏み込まなかったものの、重大な事態に備えた連絡や情報共有など、両国の利害が
一致しやすい分野では限定的な協力が具体化している。米国内でも、最先端AI の能力評価や外
国への技術流出対策など、安全保障上の管理が進められており、議会には独立評価やインシデ
ント報告を求める法案が提出されている7。さらに9 月 29 日には、Anthropic、OpenAI、Google、
Meta、xAI、NVIDIA の 6 社の代表者 がホワイトハウスで “Joint Commitment on Frontier 
Responsibilities”に署名し、内部統制の強化、外部監査人との連携、基準づくりのための定
期協議を約束した。ただし、これは法的拘束力のない自主的な取り組みであり、トランプ政権
は政府による規制よりも自主規制を重視する姿勢を改めて示した8。 
このように、AI の安全性をめぐっては、企業間・国家間で共通の対応を模索する動きが見ら
れる一方、開発ペースや規制のあり方をめぐる立場には隔たりがある（図表1）。

2. AI 脅威論の二つの側面と、安全確保と開発競争のジレンマ 
AI 脅威論①：AI の高度化そのものがもたらす脅威 
AI 脅威論の一つ目の捉え方は、AI の能力が高度化すること自体が、人間や社会に大きな被害
をもたらす可能性に注目するものである。代表的な論点として、AI が人間の意図とは異なる目
標を追求する問題や、人間がAI の判断や行動を十分に監視・制御できなくなる（制御喪失）リ
スクが挙げられる。AI が次世代 AI の開発を支援し、自身の能力向上を加速させる再帰的自己
改善が進めば、人間による評価や安全対策が追いつかなくなるとの懸念もある。もっとも、こ
うした懸念が実際に顕在化する時期や確率を見積もることは難しく、不確実性が大きい。 
一方、近年は、サイバー攻撃の支援や脆弱性の探索、生物学的な情報の生成など、AI が現時
点で持つ危険な能力を、具体的な評価や事案から把握する動きが進んでいる。2026 年夏には、
評価中の AI エージェントが外部システムに侵入する事案が複数の企業で相次いで明らかにな
り、評価環境の外部に影響を及ぼし得る能力が示された9。ただし、これを制御喪失の兆候とみ
るか、安全装置を外した評価環境における封じ込めの失敗とみるかで見方は分かれる。危険な
能力の確認が実社会での大規模な被害に直結するとは限らず、将来的な制御喪失のシナリオと、
現時点で観測される危険な能力を区別して評価する必要がある。

AI 脅威論②：AI の開発・保有・普及がもたらす脅威 
AI 脅威論の二つ目の捉え方は、AI そのものの危険性だけでなく、高度な AI を誰が開発・保
有し、誰が利用できるかが、企業や国家の力関係を変化させることに注目するものである。最
先端AI は、経済成長や情報収集、サイバー・軍事能力などに影響し得るため、その開発・保有
能力は国家安全保障上の優位にもつながり得る。このため、米中間では、AI モデルだけでなく、
半導体、計算資源、クラウド、人材などを含む開発基盤全体が競争の対象となっている。モデ
ル蒸留などをめぐる問題も、知的財産の問題にとどまらず、高度なAI 能力が競合企業や他国へ
移転する経済安全保障上の問題として捉えられている。 
危険な能力へのアクセス制限は、悪用や無制限な拡散を抑えるために必要となり得る一方、
最先端 AI を利用できる国・企業と利用できない主体との格差を広げる可能性がある。 実際、
2026 年 6 月には、米国政府がAnthropic に対して最先端モデルへの外国籍者のアクセスを全面
禁止する輸出規制措置を発出した結果、同社が対象モデルの提供を全世界で一時停止し、日本
からも一時的に利用できなくなった10。また、安全規制への対応に高度な評価能力や多額の費
用が必要になれば、既存大手への集中を強め、新規参入を妨げるおそれもある。このように、
高度なAI 能力が一部の国家・企業に集中することと、危険な能力が無制限に拡散することの双

9 Anthropic“Investigating three real-world incidents in our cybersecurity evaluations”, July 30, 
2026; OpenAI“The Hugging Face incident and the road ahead”, August 26, 2026; among others. 
10 新田尭之・田邉美穂「米国政府はなぜ最先端 AI を停止させたのか：最先端AI モデルへの輸出規制措置が示
す AI 統治の転換点」（大和総研レポート、2026 年6 月17 日）

1

5 / 8

方がリスクとなり、その能力を誰が保有し、誰が利用や移転を管理するのかが重要な論点とな
っている。

安全確保と開発競争のジレンマと協調を阻む構造 
これら二つの脅威は相互に影響している。AI の能力が高まるほど、安全対策の必要性と、そ
の能力を保有する経済的・安全保障上の価値がともに高まり、企業間・国家間の開発競争が激
しくなる。競争を優先すれば安全対策が後回しになりやすいが、単独で開発ペースを落とせば、
減速しない企業や国家が技術的な優位を得る可能性がある。このため、危険性を認識しても開
発競争から離脱しにくいというジレンマが生じる。 また、危険な能力へのアクセス制限や安全
規制は、悪用や拡散を抑える一方、能力の集中や利用格差 の拡大といった別のジレンマを生み
かねない（図表2）。 
こうした二つのジレンマへの対応には、企業や国家による協調が必要となる。ただし、AI で
は開発の主体と最先端の能力が国家ではなく民間企業に集中しているため、 協調は企業間と国
家間の二つの層で求められ、その難しさもそれぞれ異なる。 
企業間では、各社が安全対策の必要性を共有し、他社も同等の対策を実施すると確認できれ
ば、歩調を合わせる余地がある。相互の協力が期待できる状況では、協調が各社にとって最も
望ましく、抜け駆けする誘因は小さい。こうした安全対策の共通化は、協調の成否が相互の信
頼にかかるという点で、いわゆる「鹿狩りゲーム（全員が協力すれば最大の成果が得られるが、
他者の協力を信じられなければ各自が確実だが小さな成果を選んでしまう状況を表すゲーム理
論のモデル）」に近い構図である。実際、OpenAI、Anthropic、Google は、AI の安全性をめぐる
企業間協調について協議を進めている11。ただし、中国企業やオープンモデルとの競争も続い
ており、協調に加わらない主体が存在すれば、参加企業も 競争に備えざるを得ず、企業間協調
は不安定になる。 
国家間では、相手国が減速すれば抜け駆けして優位に立てる一方、相手国が開発を続ければ
減速した自国だけが後れを取る。このため、相手国の行動にかかわらず、自国は国内企業の開
発を後押しすることが合理的になりやすい。その結果、双方が減速する方が互いに望ましい場
合でも、双方が開発を続ける状態に陥りやすい。これは、いわゆる「囚人のジレンマ（各自が
自分にとって合理的な選択をした結果、全員にとって望ましくない結果に陥る状況を表すゲー
ム理論のモデル） 」に近い。合意によって減速を約束しても、各国には約束を破る誘因が残る
うえに、相手国が守っているかを確認することも難しい。国家間競争は自国企業に開発継続を
促すため、企業間の協調だけでは安全確保と開発競争のジレンマを解消できるとは限らない。

11 CNBC“OpenAI, Google, Anthropic discussing collaboration on AI safety issues”, September 15, 
2026.

図表 2 協調を阻む安全確保と開発競争のジレンマ

（出所）各種資料より大和総研作成（イラストはソコスト https://soco-st.com/） 
3. 企業間協調の可能性と限界、日本の役割 
安全性をめぐる企業間協調は機能するか 
OpenAI、Anthropic、Google DeepMind による協調は、安全対策を共通の競争条件とし、個社
だけが不利益を負う問題を緩和する可能性がある。ただし、協調の対象によって実現可能性は
異なる。能力評価の手法、第三者検証、インシデント報告などの情報共有や共通基準づくりは
比較的進めやすい。一方、開発ペースそのものの合意は競争を直接制約するため、抜け駆けの
誘因が残るほか、独占禁止法（反トラスト法）に抵触するおそれもある。実際、米国では、開
発減速をめぐる各社の呼びかけが違法な競争制限に当たるとして、消費者による集団訴訟が提
起されている12。 
前章で示した協調を阻む構造を踏まえると、企業間協調が機能するには、①主要企業の参加、
②検証の信頼性、③副作用の抑制という三つの条件が求められる。 
一つ目の主要企業の参加では、主要な開発主体が協調に参加し、不参加企業との競争に対処
できることが求められる。不参加主体が開発を続ければ、参加企業にも安全策を緩める誘因が
生じるため、協調の参加範囲を広げるとともに、不参加主体との競争によって合意が崩れない
仕組みが必要となる。 
二つ目の検証の信頼性では、合意した安全策の実施状況を相互に確認できることが求められ
る。他社が合意を守っていると確認できなければ、自社だけが不利益を負うことへの警戒から、
協調は維持されにくい。ただし、最先端モデルを評価できる主体は限られるほか、評価期間の

短縮や、評価中のモデルが評価の意図を認識して行動を変える可能性など、検証の信頼性を損
なう要因もある。 
三つ目の副作用の抑制では、協調が市場集中や競争制限につながることを防ぐ必要がある。
前章でみたように、高度で費用のかかる安全基準は既存大手への集中を強めかねない。また、
政府や評価機関が主要企業の知見や資源に依存すれば、企業の利益が過度に制度に反映される
おそれもある。このため、基準形成の透明性や外部主体の関与を確保し、安全性を理由とする
協調が既存企業の地位を守り、市場競争を制限する手段とならないようにする必要がある。 
企業間の自主的な合意だけでは、不参加企業に網をかけられず、検証の独立性や基準づくり
の透明性も確保しにくい。このため、三つの条件を満たしていくには、企業間で形成された安
全基準や評価方法を各国の制度に取り込み、国際的な枠組みを通じて共有する必要がある 。国
家間で開発減速に合意するなど、開発ペースそのものを調整することは難しくとも、能力評価
やインシデント報告の基準を共有し、各国が共通の基準に基づいて企業の対応を検証する仕組
みは構築し得る。 
こうした仕組みの実効性を確保するには、企業間で形成された基準を国際的に共有するだけ
でなく、各国の言語や利用環境において安全策が機能するかを確認し、その実施状況を独立し
た立場から検証できる主体を増やす必要がある。

日本は国際的な枠組みづくりで存在感を示せるか 
最先端 AI の開発を米国企業などが先導する中でも、日本が AI の安全性をめぐる国際的な枠
組みの形成に関与する余地は残されている。日本は、2023 年 5 月の G7 広島サミットを契機に
立ち上げられた広島 AI プロセスを通じて、高度な AI を開発する組織に求める国際指針や行動
規範の形成を主導してきた13。広島 AI プロセスでは、国際行動規範への対応状況を各組織が自
主的に報告・公表する「報告枠組み」も運用されており、各社のリスク管理や安全対策を国際
的に可視化・比較するための基盤が形成されている 14。ただし、企業間で形成された安全基準
を各国の制度へ接続する段階では、こうした原則や枠組みを提唱した実績だけで、日本が引き
続き国際的な枠組み形成に実質的に関与できるとは限らない。 
前節で整理したとおり、企業間で形成された安全基準や評価方法を国際的な枠組みへ発展さ
せるには、各国の言語や利用環境における実効性を 独立した立場から検証できる主体を増やす
必要がある。評価・検証に必要なのは、評価対象モデルへのアクセス、評価手法や評価環境、
専門人材であり、自国で最先端AI を開発する能力を有することは、必ずしも前提とならない。
日本が国際的な枠組みの形成に実質的に関与するには、まずは最先端AI の能力・リスクに関す
る評価結果や、安全対策の実効性を自ら技術的に検証できる体制を整えることが重要となる。 
日本には、その足掛かりとなる実績や知見がある。日本政府が 2024 年に設立した、AI の安

14 広島 AI プロセス「報告枠組み」（2026 年10 月5 日最終閲覧）

全性に関する評価や基準策定を担う機関、AI セーフティ・インスティテュート（AISI）15は、
10 か国が参加するAI モデルの国際共同テスト演習に加わり、複数の言語におけるLLM（大規模
言語モデル）の評価手法や AI エージェントの評価手法の改善に取り組んできた16。日本が知見
を蓄積しつつある領域である。
このように、日本には評価・検証の足掛かりとなる実績があるものの、それを本格的に担う
ための体制整備はこれからである。AISI はこれまで、評価基準やガイドといった「物差しづく
り」を重視してきた。2025 年の年次レポートでは、今後の方向性として「自ら評価する能力を
もつ」ことと「国際協調を主導する」ことを掲げている17。日本政府も 2026 年 7 月に改定・閣
議決定した「人工知能基本計画」において、AISI の抜本的強化を引き続き掲げている。同年 6
月には、将来的に AISI が最先端 AI などを評価できる体制の構築に向け、必要な手法や評価環
境を検討する調査が開始された18。

日本に期待されるのは、企業間で形成された安全基準をそのまま受け入れるのではなく、各
国の言語や利用環境における実効性を検証し、国際的な枠組みへ接続する際の橋渡し役である。
そのためには、AISI の専門人材や評価環境の充実に加え、公開前を含む評価対象モデルへのア
クセス、機微情報を扱うための情報管理体制、他国のAI 安全機関との共同評価体制を整える必
要がある。AI の能力向上と企業間協調をめぐる議論が急速に進む中、最先端 AI を自ら評価す
る日本の体制づくりはなお調査・検討段階にある。このままでは、最先端 AI の開発だけでな
く、その安全性をめぐる国際的な枠組みの形成でも後れを取りかねない。広島AI プロセスで培
った実績を活かし、国際的な枠組みづくりに継続的に関与していくためにも、最先端 AI の能
力・リスクに関する評価結果や安全対策の実効性を、自ら技術的に検証できる体制を早急に整
えることが求められる。

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-09-17_How the AI supercycle is reshaping global interest rates_f60e61d1.pdf

In Summary 
• The AI supercycle will pull long -term yields down in both the US and Europe , but for 
very different reasons and by very different paths. We model this across five transmission 
channels – three macro (neutral rate, inflation expectations, fiscal collateral) and two 
micro (AI -driven duration supply and crowding -out) – applied to an AI upside and 
downside scenario. Over a 10-year horizon the net effect is around −50bp for both regions 
in the upside scenario (roughly +5pp of cumulated US potential growth, +1.6pp for the 
eurozone), and a prolonged, smaller drag in the downside (−2.5pp and −1.3pp 
respectively). The US result depends on AI producti vity actually materializing to keep its 
fiscal trajectory intact; the eurozone gets to the same place even without the AI dividend, 
since its fiscal position doesn't need repairing in the first place. The US is the levered, 
volatile expression of the AI trade; Europe is the safer one. 
• The market has already priced the AI productivity story for the US, but not the fiscal 
relief that should come with it. The USD 5y1y forward has moved from ~3.0% to 4.9% since 
mid-2025 some 150bp above our median neutral -rate estimate and 90bp above the top  
of our estimate range, and more than the full +67bp our model assigns to the Ai enhanced 
neutral-rate range over the next decade. What isn't priced at all is the fiscal collateral 
improvement, worth −85bp to the US 10Y by the end of the horizon. Either th e market 
doesn't believe AI will repair the fiscal trajectory, or it's pricing a less favorable version of 
the same story with higher AI -driven job displacement pushing up social protection and 
unemployment spending enough to offset the tax-revenue gain. 
• Fiscal collateral – the extent to which a stronger economy makes a government's debt 
easier to repay – is what actually decides the outcome, in both scenarios and both 
regions. The transatlantic rates converging seems counterintuitive at first view as Europe's 
growth impulse is a third the size of the US. But its fiscal position benefits more, because 
the improvement lands on a stable debt path rather than the expansionary US pat h. For 
every point of growth AI delivers, Europe's fiscal position improves abou t 1.6 times more 
than that of the US. 
• The way AI interacts with interest rate isn't a straight path, and its shape differs on each 
side of the Atlantic.  Both curves rise well above their eventual resting point before 
reversing, the US 10Y's round trip is 135bp (versus 80bp for the eurozone) even though the 
net 10 -year effect is only −50bp for both. The peak trade also looks different in each 
market: a butterfly in the US (buy the belly, sell the wings, as front-loaded micro effects hit 
the wings while macro effects lag into the belly) versus a flattener in Europe. By the end of 
the period both curves converge to a similar, milder flattening.

How the AI supercycle is reshaping global interest 
rates

### Page 2

AI is no longer only a sector story, but a macro-scale claim on the economy. 
Artificial intelligence (AI) has become a major macroeconomic force. AI investment will pass USD1tn globally in 
2026, of which more than USD500bn in the US alone . That is 1.8% of US GDP and 0.9% of world GDP, on a path to 
2.8% and 1.4% respectively by 2028.  In terms of growth, tech and data center activity will represent 36%-50% of US 
year-on-year growth this year. Globally, the direct AI investment impulse is worth roughly 10% of global growth. 
From the financial markets side,  AI has mostly been examined through the lens of  equities (sector analysis , 
valuation, concentration risk), or private credit  (risk profile and risk pricing of AI investments types (see our latest 
reports here linked here). This paper asks how the AI supercycle transmits into interest rates .. 
US and eurozone vary in the level of macro impact, but share similar profile 
We model the effect against a baseline without AI effects, measuring the difference versus an AI  upside scenario 
and one in which AI shifts to the downside. We use several published estimates to derive scenarios for the impact 
on potential growth, trend productivity and trend employment. We define an  upside scenario  with cumulated 
potential growth gains of +4.94pp in the US and +1.60pp in the eurozone; in the downside we assume -2.47pp in the 
US and -1.35pp in the eurozone. The US macro scenario shows much greater sensitivity to AI, while Europe has less 
to gain in terms of productivity . The pattern is similar for inflation, where in the upside scenario the US face s a 
stronger supply- and energy-driven uptick, before higher productivity gains take over and reverse the AI impact into 
a disinflationary trend. Inversely in the negative scenario, the negative AI demand shock brings US inflation further 
down than it does in the eurozone (Table 1).

Our framework rests on five major transmission channels 
We identify two major channels of transmission: 1) a macroeconomic channel through potential growth, inflation 
dynamics and the fiscal position (plus a spillover effect for the eurozone), and 2) a microeconomic channel through 
the changes AI financing brings to the structure of fixed income markets. The latter has gained importance recently 
as market participants have discussed the possibility that AI debt issuance was competing with US government debt 
and therefore contributing to driving up yields. The macro channels capture the changes in the way long-term rates 
discount economic variable s. The micro channels c apture how the AI funding model (market size, self-funded vs 
debt issuance) affects the price of duration. Macro and micro channels work on different lags, and their effects can 
change sign over our 10-year forecast horizon. Generally, the macro channels affect the expected rate component 
of long-term rates, while micro channels act on the term premium. The exception is the fiscal position which, despite 
being a macro channel, affects the term premium component of long term government bond yields.  
1) Neutral rate r*  is the real policy rate at which the economy neither overheats nor stalls. It is the level the 
expected path of short rates is anchored to. If AI raises trend productivity, capital earns more, firms want to 
invest more, and the neutral rate balancing saving against investment rises. A lasting productivity gain 
therefore lifts r*  which feeds through the entire yield curve. It has  a stronger effect at the front end as it 
transmits through expected short rates, and productivity gains have a long-term mean reverting trend. The 
effect is inverted in case AI would fail to produce productivity gains in an economy facing a debt overhang 
from AI investments.  
2) For inflation expectations we use an adaptive expectation approach where the estimation windows adjust 
with previous estimation errors. As AI has a strong supply impact at first, we get upside push in expectations

### Page 3

3

that fades with time when productivity gains take over . As the phase in becomes smoother, the change in 
expectation is less pronounced to the downside. These factors also influence the expected rate component 
of government bond yields. 
3) Fiscal collateral is defining the fundamental value  of sovereign bonds as discounted claims on future 
primary surpluses. The channel captures its change in response to an AI-driven up- or downside productivity 
shock. A productivity shock has an asymmetric effect: tax revenues improving the fiscal position are elastic 
to GDP, while the largest spending lines (pensions, benefits, debt service) are sticky.  
4) Duration supply  is a market -microstructure model tracking AI capex as a generator of new duration, 
through both long -dated IG bonds and pay -fixed swap structures in private credit. The curve effect sits at 
the long end  as 65% the supply lands beyond 10 years. The swap  spread effect  depends on fixed-rate 
issuance, which  cheapens the sovereign against swaps  while p ay-fixed hedging from priv ate credit, 
enrichens it. Duration supply affects the term premium, it is more pronounced at the longer end of the curve. 
5) Crowding Out is a sector -level displacement model affecting long -term rates via the swap-market. 
Financial institutions issue fixed-rate bonds and then ask for floating, receiving fixed in swaps. That receive-
fixed leg is how financial issuance moves duration out of the sovereign market. AI borrowers sit on the 
opposite side of the swap curve, paying fixed rather than receiving it . So both channels lift swap yields 
against the sovereign: one adds fixed -payer supply, the other removes fixed -receiver demand . Like 
duration supply, crowding-out affects the term premium at the longer end of the curve. 
6) Spillover Effect is added for the eurozone, acting mainly at the long end, where global term premia and 
rate levels remain structurally correlated and anchored to the US benchmark. It captures how AI effects 
propagate to global fixed income markets via the US Treasury market, even where AI exposur e is limited.

### Page 4

The AI super cycle is a bull market for duration, but it is not a directional trade 
Our results show that AI is a bull market for duration in both scenarios, but the path matters. In the upside scenario, 
AI lowers long-term rates by around 50bp over a 10-year horizon in both the US and the eurozone. In the downside 
scenario, rates are also held down for a prolonged period. In both cases, however, yields rise before they fall: the 
US 10Y makes a 135bp round trip in the upside and 72bp in the downside, against 80bp and 41bp for the eurozone. 
The AI effect on rates is therefore not a long-term directional trade, the right timing is essential. The timing depends 
on which channels dominate when, how long they take to act and where on the curve they bite hardest. That makes 
the shape of the curve as important as the yield level for positioning (Figure 1). 
Figure 1: Net AI effect on the 10Y yield – Upside and Downside scenarios 
in basis points

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

The end points for the US and the eurozone are remarkably close, even though the two start from very different 
positions in the AI race. The fiscal collateral channel explains why. Europe gets only a moderate growth impulse, 
but it lands on a stable fiscal position, which improves the value of its fiscal collateral significantly. The US gets a 
much larger growth impulse, yet the gain is partly offset by continued fiscal expansion over the next 10 years (we 
use the CBO forecast of debt/GDP rising 20pp over the next 10 years ). With a stable debt path, the increase in US 
fiscal collateral  value would subtract 125bp rather than 85 bp, lifting the total effect to -90bp and leaving the 
eurozone far behind. The transatlantic AI rates convergence is thus a late-cycle outcome: at the peak, the AI effect 
is more than twice as large in the US as in the eurozone.

The US curve rotates between the peak and the end of period due to micro effects 
At the peak of the upside scenario, the US curve shifts up, but with the middle of the curve (belly) holding up better: 
the 2s30s slope barely moves while 2s5s flattens 13bp and 5s30s steepens 12bp. The peak AI trade on the US curve 
is therefore a butterfly (buy the belly, sell the wings). In the eurozone the peak AI effect on the curve is a flattener 
instead: 2s30s −20bp, with the 2Y up 51bp against the 30Y up 31bp. This shape difference is driven by the stronger 
and front-loaded US micro channels, set against macro channels that arrive with a lag. In addition to the time lag, 
macro channels (neutral rate, inflation expectations) act mainly at the short end, while the micro channels hit the 
long end because AI issuance is long -dated. The 5Y sits in between: it absorbs little of the duration -supply and 
crowding-out pressure, yet is long enough to benefit from the improving fiscal collateral effect on its term premium. 
By the end of the period, both curves flatten to a similar degree  as fiscal collateral value becomes the dominant 
force, pulling the belly and the long end down while the 2Y is barely affected  (US 2s30s −41bp, EA −3 0bp) In the

Microeconomic channels and inflation move first, the fiscal channel decides the end point 
Having looked at the  way AI effect the  changes in yield levels and the influences the shape of the curve s, we now 
turn to the decomposition of the moves : which channels drives the yield and curve dynamics, how much each 
contributes and when. The sequence is the same in both scenarios: micro channels  and inflation expectations drive 
rates early on, while the fiscal channel and the neutral rate r* only kick in later.  
In the upside scenario, the peak arrives when productivity gains lift r* while inflation expectations are still elevated 
and the micro channels are still at work but before higher growth has significantly fed through to tax revenues rising 
the value of the fiscal collateral. For the US at the peak in 2032, r* alone contributes +67bp against −18bp from the 
fiscal channel. By the end point the picture reverses: the fiscal channel contributes −85bp, r* slows down to +42bp 
and inflation expectations flip to −18bp as the disinflationary effect of productivity gains sets in. Europe follows the 
same sequence at roughly half the amplitude, reflecting its lower sensitivity to AI.  
In the downside scenario, rates first move up, driven by inflation expectations on the back of AI investment and 
energy demand. In the US, the micro channels add another 20 -30bp from AI-related issuance. The tipping point is 
in 2031: productivity gains fail to materialize and the debt overhang starts weighing on growth, which later erodes 
tax revenues, increases government spending and reduces value of the fiscal collateral. In the eurozone the micro 
channels are negligible, but the spillover from US rates matters. 
The greater US sensitivity also shows up in the gross channel activity , i.e. the sum of all channel contributions in 
absolute terms. In the upside it amounts to 158bp in the US against 78bp in Europe for a broadly similar final 
outcome. In other words, much more happens under the surface in the US, but most of it cancels out: only 30% of the 
US channel activity is left once the channels are netted against each other, compared with 58% in Europe. The AI 
journey is therefore likely to be bumpier for US rates than for eurozone rates, even if both end up in a similar place. 
The AI effect should therefore translate into relatively higher US rates volatility  (Figure 4 & 5). 
Figure 4: Channel contributions to the 10Y  - Upside and Downside scenario 
in basis points

### Page 7

Figure 5: Channel contribution at every point of the curve: peak and terminal (basis points) 
In basis points

The fiscal channel has the last word – and the US needs the AI dividend more than Europe 
We find no transatlantic divide in the long-term AI effect on rates. At the end of the period the US and eurozone 10Y 
effects are only a few basis points apart in both scenarios. But the path to that point differs materially in level, drivers 
and timing. The convergence of the end points is attributable almost entirely to fiscal channel. Fiscal collateral 
contributes −85bp to the US terminal effect in the upside and −46bp to the eurozone, in both cases more than the 
channels working in the opposite direction, principally the neutral rate r* effect. 
The mechanism is the productivity beta of government debt. A positive productivity shock raises tax revenues by 
more than the GDP gain, so the present value of future primary surpluses improves and the bondholder is effectively 
long AI productivity. Europe is disadvantaged here, as it tax revenues are less sensitive to GDP (also because AI 
growth is tilted versus US and less taxable by European governments) but its spending is also more sticky, as Europe 
is more exposed to negative labor market effects caused by AI. On the other hand, the fiscal trajectory of Europe is 
more favorable, with a rather stable debt to GDP ratio projection of 86% for the next 10 years. Over the same time 
period, the CBO sees US debt growing to 20pp of GDP. 
What differs between the two markets is the debt path on which that improvement lands: deteriorating in the US, 
stable in the eurozone. The US is therefore the more dependent of the two on the AI productivity call actually being 
realized, as it needs the additional revenue to sustain its current expansionary fiscal stance, whereas the eurozone 
would maintain its debt trajectory even without the AI dividend.  
On our calibration, Europe’s fiscal-collateral beta is 1.6× the US per point of growth delivered, notwithstanding the 
more favorable US elasticities (higher revenue elasticity, lower spending elasticity). The difference lies in the 
denominator: a smaller debt stock (EUR1 4tn against USD 40tn), a lower starting yield, a lower baseline real rate

and a flat baseline debt path against a deteriorating one, with the CBO projecting US debt rising 20pp of GDP by

advantage would widen further (Figure 6).

The neutral rate: bigger in the US, more decisive in Europe 
The r* channel is larger in the US but more decisive in Europe. In the US upside it is the biggest single positive 
contribution at both moments, + 67bp at the 2032 peak and +42bp at the 2036 end point, but it sits inside 158bp of 
gross channel activity and is therefore largely netted away by the other channels. In the eurozone it is effectively 
the only positive channel left at the end of the period, at +15bp. Both paths rise with a delay, starting two to three 
years from now. The US path runs much higher but also mean -reverts more strongly later on, so the gap to the 
eurozone, which follows a smoother path, narrows over time. In the downside the paths are less pronounced and 
the two regions are closer together: the absolute impact is stronger in the US, while the eurozone shows the higher 
sensitivity relative to its own starting point.

AI issuance is affecting the US treasury market, the euro market is untouched for now 
The micro channels are a US story: the US dominates AI-related debt issuance and duration supply. In the eurozone 
the effect is still small, with EUR-denominated AI issuance accounting for around 1% of the corporate benchmark

indices and 10% share of global AI-related debt issuance. But it could change if reverse -Yankee AI bond issuance 
picks up in the coming years. On our estimates, however, euro issuance volumes would have to be around 10 times 
higher, increasing by min 20-25% p.a. to move eurozone rates to a comparable degree. Direct duration supply 
remains the dominant one at the peak. Together they add 34bp to the US 10Y at the peak, against 25bp today, and 
both steepen the curve as they act mainly on longer maturities. The effect on asset swap spreads (ASW) is positive 
through the direct pay-fixed leg, but negative through the crowding-out leg (Figure 8). 
Figure 8: Peak impact of AI micro economic channels on 10y yields, slope and ASW 
In basis points

* based on De Vere, Ramaswamy & Searls (Dallas Fed, Feb 2026) 
Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
The market prices the AI productivity gain, but not the fiscal relief that should come with it 
In the US, the rates market has already fully priced the productivity leg of the AI story. The 5y1y forward swap has 
climbed from 3.0% in mid-2025 to 4.9%, against a median neutral-rate estimate of 3.4% and range of 2.7-4.0%, which 
puts the market -implied neutral rate some 1 50bp above the median and 90bp above the top of the band. Our AI 
models would add +67bp to current estimates pushing the range to 3.3-4.67% – a range the market already exceeds. 
What is missing from the current A I narrative pricing is the other side of the same story: the fiscal collateral 
improvement that these productivity gains should bring, worth −85bp on the US 10Y  over the next ten years. That 
leg arrives with a much longer lag, as it takes a visible turn in tax receipts to shift the narrative (Figure 9). We would 
therefore read the current level as a temporary mispricing rather than a challenge to the fiscal leg of the AI story. 
The front-loading of the productivity channel builds up correction potential.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
As was universally expected, the RBA Monetary Policy
Board (MPB) lifted the cash rate by 25bps to 4.6% at its
September 2026 meeting.
This is quite the turnaround from the 11 August meeting,
where an extended period on hold looked to be the base case.

At that time, the data had broken against the RBA’s more
hawkish view, and its published forecasts did not support
further hikes. But the MPB can now point to the emergence of
some of the upside risks to inflation it warned of in August.
The post-meeting statement was notable for its emphasis
on the Middle East conflict, noting that “global energy prices
are now much higher than had been assumed in the August
forecasts” – “much higher” is stronger language than one

normally sees in an RBA statement. The RBA’s liaison indicates
that firms are passing this on or planning to do so. The pass-
through was characterised as partial, and additional to
domestically driven inflation arising from capacity pressures. We
read this as implying that the RBA is concerned about upside
risks to underlying inflation in Q4 of this year, as well as Q3.
The RBA is also wary about pressure on both construction
costs and retail prices of IT goods arising from the AI/data

centre boom, although in the media conference, the Governor
clarified that risks there were building rather than materialising.
The broader background highlighted in the Governor’s opening
statement to the media conference, is of demand outstripping
supply. The statement evidenced this by noting the stronger
July CPI and June quarter GDP results than expected.
The accompanying statement was notable for the absence of
language about the labour market being a little tight, despite

this being the inter-meeting message from the Governor
and other staff (see, for example, the Governor’s opening
statement on 18 September) and the language in the post-
meeting media conference. Views clearly differ on this: we
note that the Treasury forecasts underlying the May budget
and the recent Intergenerational Review imply both more
spare capacity in the labour market (a lower NAIRU) and faster
growth in potential from wherever the starting point is (higher

productivity growth and trend increase in participation).
Instead, the post-meeting statement characterised the
labour market as easing “broadly as expected”, despite the
unemployment rate already rising higher than the August SMP
forecast of 4.5% for the December quarter.
RBA hikes in September, November follow-up now expected
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
The decision was unanimous, suggesting that any qualms
external MPB members might have had about the RBA’s

downbeat view of supply capacity were overruled by resurgent
oil prices and the stronger than forecast CPI and GDP data.
With upside risks being seen to have emerged, the post-meeting
statement language included that the Board “will continue to
do what it considers necessary to bring inflation sustainably
back to target, including increasing the cash rate target further if
needed”, a small but meaningful change from the “if upside risks
materialise” language from the previous meeting, and back to

the tone of the language at the June meeting.
The bar for a follow-up hike in November is low. Indeed,
judging by the post-meeting rhetoric, a November hike is now
the base case, absent a lasting resolution of the Middle East
conflict beforehand, or some other event that significantly
lowers the outlook for energy-related costs in Australia. The
bar for hikes beyond that is much higher, given the cumulative
rise in interest rates, and noting that the labour market is

easing and the housing market will likely weaken further.
The August inflation data released the day after the meeting
was in line with our expectations. We therefore continue to
expect Q3 trimmed mean inflation around 0.9%qtr, too high for
the RBA’s comfort, and upside risks for Q4. Likewise, there are
no implications for our rates view from this week’s inflation data.
Forecast changes
• We have updated our financial forecasts to reflect the change

in our rate view.
• Our labour market forecasts are unchanged on the first
decimal place, but there is some modest upside risk to our
unemployment forecast.
• Our updated GDP profile can be found on page 9.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: searching for signal
led the correction to date, the fact that the other capitals
are also reporting price declines clearly indicates that the
correction is deepening and broadening, as higher interest
rates collide with the tax policy changes announced in May’s

budget. Sentiment effects indeed remain at play, as evinced
by the continued pull-back in investor credit growth . Westpac
anticipates a peak-to-trough decline of around 7%, with tight
on-market supply limiting the extent of the correction. For
more, see our latest Housing Pulse.
Offshore, there was plenty of data to dissect.
In the US, inflation eased in August despite ongoing strength
in household demand and higher energy prices. Headline PCE
inflation rose 0.3%mth and 3.4%yr, while core PCE increased

0.2%mth and 3.0%yr. Encouragingly for the FOMC, the
share of PCE components rising by more than 3% over the
past year, a measure frequently cited by Fed Chairman Kevin
Warsh, declined to 45% from 49%, suggesting some easing in
broad-based inflation pressures. At the same time, household
spending remained robust, increasing 0.9%mth, equivalent
to around 0.6%mth in real terms. That said, personal income
growth was soft, rising 0.2% in the month, signalling

households opted to draw down savings.
The ISM manufacturing survey was stronger than the
headline suggested. While the index edged down 0.1pt to
54.5, manufacturing has now expanded for nine consecutive
months. There were some signs of capacity constraints
emerging, with production softened in the month but backlogs
up 4.6pts to 56.4, suggesting that firms struggled to keep
pace with demand. New orders rose 1.6pts to 55.3, primarily
due to domestic demand as new export orders eased, backlogs

increased. Firms responded by lifting employment, 1.5pts to
52.7. Against this backdrop manufacturers saw a renewed
acceleration in cost pressures, with the prices paid index
jumping 6.8pts to 77.9. Firms cited higher steel and aluminium
prices, elevated energy costs, rising freight charges and
ongoing semiconductor shortages, with every major industry
reporting higher input costs during the month.
The past week’s data will have done nothing to bridge the

clear divide across the FOMC, as evident in this week’s busy
Fedspeak. Some participants argued for further rate hikes,
citing strong inflation, diminished supply capacity and policy
that is not yet restrictive. Others counselled patience, pointing
to progress on inflation and that September’s hike buys time
to assess upcoming data. The one thing both agree on is that
inflation will be the primary determinant of the timing and
extent of further rate hikes. Our view remains that one further

hike is likely this cycle to bring policy closer to neutral.
In Australia, this week’s main event was the RBA Monetary
Policy Board’s (MPB) decision to raise the cash rate by 25bps
to 4.60%. This outcome was universally expected given
Governor Bullock had already communicated that upside risks
to inflation had materialised – the conditionality established
by the MPB back in August for considering another rate hike.
Governor Bullock made explicit note of three upside risks.

The Middle East conflict was emphasised as the risk that had
“materialised”, given the re-escalation and renewed surge in crude
oil and refined product prices over recent months. The RBA’s
liaison warns this could lead to further pass-through of costs
and more persistent inflation pressures. On top of this, domestic
capacity pressures and the data centre/AI investment boom were
also noted as upside risks that the MPB remain alert to.
In a video update mid-week, Chief Economist Luci Ellis

explains why the bar for a follow-up rate hike in November is
low. We now expect a 25bp increase in November, provided
there is no sudden resolution of the Middle East conflict
beforehand, or some other event that significantly lowers the
outlook for energy-related costs.
Subsequently, the August CPI revealed that underlying
(trimmed mean) inflation rose 0.2% (3.4%yr), which was
slightly below the market’s expectation but spot on Westpac’s
forecast. As usual, the monthly detail was mixed, but the key

drivers of underlying inflation remain centred on housing-
related components, particularly rents and new dwelling
purchases. Headline inflation remained volatile, with the
unwind of the fuel excise cut causing another spike in fuel
prices. We have maintained our forecast for a 0.9% lift in
trimmed mean inflation in Q3, and with upside risks building
for Q4, a follow-up rate hike in November appears most likely.
Regarding the current growth pulse, household spending

surprised with a flat reading in August. Services spend fell back
following recent strength that was driven by major sporting
events, but this was offset by an increase in goods spend,
particularly in fuel and electric vehicles. Together with our
latest card data for early September, the consumer spending
pulse is shading a touch softer. However, another solid
outcome for business investment could once again be on the
cards for Q3. August’s goods trade data showcased another

significant spike in data centre-related equipment imports, up
$4.3bn, similar in scale to Q1. While clearly import-driven, our
modelling suggests that the local economic impact of such
investment is likely to be around half of the total investment.
Before moving offshore, a final note on housing. Cotality’s
latest update revealed that dwelling prices fell another 1.1%
nationally in September. While Sydney and Melbourne have
Sian Fenner, Head of Business and Industry Economics

Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist

4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
In China, policymakers announced another round of targeted
support measures aimed at stabilising growth and supporting
the struggling property sector. The PBOC lowered rates on its
Pledged Supplementary Lending facility by 25bps to 1.5% and
expanded quotas for targeted lending programs. Authorities

also introduced subsidies for eligible first-home buyers. The
subsidy applies to homes with a floor area of 120m² or less
and a value below CNY1.5mn, with support capped at 1ppt of
mortgage costs, up to CNY10,000 and for a maximum term of
five years. The measures complement reforms announced in
August to improve developer financing conditions and support
housing completion. However, weak credit demand and
confidence remains a significant constraint, suggesting policy

transmission is still limited.
The September PMI data pointed to a modest improvement
in activity, although the details were less convincing.
Manufacturing returned to expansionary territory, rising 0.9pts
to 50.1, supported by stronger production and new orders.
Much of the improvement, however, reflected a rebound in
prices, with the raw materials price index rising 7.6pts and
producer prices increasing 6.2pts. Labour market conditions
remained weak, with the employment index falling a further

0.6pts and remaining below 50 for almost four years. Conditions
in the non-manufacturing sector also improved, with the
services PMI lifting from 49.0 to 50.2, driven largely by stronger
construction activity. Input and output prices moved higher,
selling prices returned back above 50, export orders improved
to 48.5 and employment increased 0.5pts, although at 45.9 it
remains firmly in contractionary territory. Overall, the surveys
suggest activity is stabilising and policy support is gaining some

traction, but weak labour demand, subdued credit growth and
soft domestic demand indicate the recovery remains fragile.
In Japan, the September Tankan survey delivered another
constructive signal on the economy and reinforced the case
for further policy normalisation. Business conditions improved
by 2pts for large manufacturers and 5pts more broadly, while
profitability indicators strengthened and capital expenditure
intentions remained elevated. Large manufacturers expect

fixed investment to increase by 11.6% in FY2026, while large
non-manufacturers expect investment growth of 11.2%,
pointing to continued confidence in the medium-term outlook.
Capacity indicators improved further across both
manufacturing and non-manufacturing sectors, with measures
of excess production capacity becoming less negative
and indicating that demand remains sufficiently strong to
keep pressure on existing capacity. Labour shortages also

intensified, with employment conditions becoming more
negative across most industries and firm sizes, pointing to a
progressively tighter labour market. Inflation expectations
remained anchored around 2%, providing further evidence
that Japan is moving towards a more sustainable inflation
environment. Overall, the survey was consistent with an
economy operating close to capacity and supports the view
that a December rate hike remains a realistic possibility.

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
